---------- turn 17 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------
========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F98.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess what brings me in is… I just can’t seem to keep things in order anymore, especially at work. I’m in laundry and dry cleaning, and there’s always tickets and receipts and little notes from the workshop, and customer jobs coming in, and somehow they all end up piled together. Then I’m standing there not knowing what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to have my little way of doing things. I mean, everybody at work has their own system, you know, like one lady keeps everything clipped together with these colored clips—she’s very particular about it—but mine just turns into a mess before I even realize it. So… yeah, that’s why I came. It’s getting hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble keeping things organized at home as well, or is this mainly a problem at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work that made me come in, I think. I mean, at home I have the usual piles like anybody, mail on the counter and that sort of thing, but at work it’s worse because there are tickets, receipts, little notes from the workshop, customer jobs coming in… and they all just end up in one pile.\n\nThen I’m looking at it like, okay, what was I supposed to do first? And someone will ask about an order, and I’m digging through papers. The other day I found a receipt stuck under a stack of cleaning slips—well, I think it was a receipt, or maybe a workshop note, that’s the problem. It all blends together. So yes, mainly work, that’s where it’s really causing trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while trying to work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll sit down to go through the dry-cleaning orders for the day, and at first I’m fine, I’m checking the slips and the labels and all that. But then a few minutes in, my mind just… wanders off. I’ll start thinking about the labels themselves, or something with choir plans, or what I need to do when I get home.\n\nAnd then I sort of snap back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking. It’s frustrating because I was looking right at it, you know? But I wasn’t really taking it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s bouncing around all the time or anything. Sitting still… well, it depends, I guess. At work I’m usually on my feet anyway, moving between the counter and the back, so I don’t really notice it there.\n\nIf I’m sitting at home, I might shift around or mess with something in my hands, but I don’t know if that’s more than anyone else. My sister does this thing where she taps her nails on the table, and it drives me crazy, so I try not to do things like that. But… yeah, I’m not sure I’ve paid much attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed by your difficulties with organization and distraction?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Overwhelmed maybe, sometimes, when everything is stacked up and I can’t tell what’s what. But then I think, well, everybody gets behind at work, right?\n\nIt’s more like I get irritated with myself. Like I’ll be staring at the pile and then remember I was supposed to call someone back, and then—oh, that reminds me, we had this one customer last week who kept changing what day she wanted pickup, and it threw everything off. So then the whole counter was full of slips, and I kept thinking I’d sort it after lunch, but lunch came and went.\n\nSo… yes, maybe overwhelmed at times, but I’m not sure if I’d say anxious. I’m not really sure where the line is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the time. I mean, I have days where I’m just worn out or in a mood, especially if work’s been a mess, but sad like all day, every day? I wouldn’t really say that.\n\nSometimes I get annoyed with myself more than anything. Like yesterday I meant to stop for groceries and then somehow ended up coming straight home, and then I was mad because I had to figure out dinner with nothing in the house. But that’s not exactly sad, I guess. So… no, I don’t think sad most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with organization and staying focused, or is this something that's developed over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly. I don’t know if it was exactly the same as now, but even back in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright but scattered. That was the phrase they used a lot, I think.\n\nThey’d say I was talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d lose papers, or I needed reminders to finish my work. My mother would get so frustrated because she’d say, “You know how to do it, why didn’t you turn it in?” And I did know how, usually, but then the paper would be in my desk or my bag or… who knows.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think it just suddenly developed. It’s kind of always been part of me, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. Not like I’m doing wild things, but I jump in too fast. Like my daughter will be asking me something and I’m already answering before she’s finished, and then she’ll say, “That’s not what I asked,” and I realize I was answering what I thought she meant.\n\nIt happens with customers too. They start asking about a pickup or a stain or whatever, and I’ll jump in with the answer before they get the whole sentence out. And then sometimes I’ve answered the wrong thing and have to back up. Same with the trainer at work—though training is hard anyway because they explain things in all these steps, and there was this one machine that kept beeping last week—anyway, yes, I do tend to answer before I’ve really heard the whole question.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with organization and focus affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I think. More at work and church than with friends, maybe, but it spills over.\n\nAt the shop, when I’m not organized, orders can run late or I mix up little details, like billing things, and then everybody’s tense because the customer’s waiting and the staff are looking at me like, “What happened?” And I hate that feeling. It makes me want to explain myself, but then I probably make it longer than it needs to be.\n\nAnd at church too—I used to help lead some of the planning stuff, like for events, and I’ve kind of stepped back because people were getting frustrated with me. Not in a mean way, exactly, but you can tell when folks are tired of reminding you or fixing things. So yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a day off work or cancel plans due to feeling overwhelmed by your disorganization or difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve taken a whole day off just for that, exactly… I mean, sometimes I’ve wanted to. At the shop it can build up, you know? Like one order is late, then the billing is wrong on another one, and then a customer is standing there waiting and the staff are all tense, and I’m trying to fix it but I can’t seem to get everything straight in my head.\n\nAnd then at church, I actually did step back from helping lead some of the planning things, because people were getting frustrated with me. Not yelling or anything, just… you can feel it when people are tired of reminding you or cleaning up after the mix-ups. So I guess I have canceled or stepped away from plans more than taken a sick day, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family or friends it’s not always as obvious as at work, but it does affect people around me. At the shop, when I’m disorganized, orders have been late, or I’ve gotten billing mixed up, and then everybody gets tense—staff, customers, me too. It kind of follows me home because I’m still upset about it.\n\nAnd at church, I stepped back from leading some of the planning things because people were getting frustrated with me. Not like anyone was cruel, but you can tell when they’re tired of fixing things or reminding you. So… yes, it has affected relationships, even if it shows up more in those settings than just sitting with a friend over coffee or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your difficulties with organization and focus have held you back from achieving your goals or doing things you want to do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel like it’s held me back. I don’t know if I’d say it in some big dramatic way, but at the shop, when orders end up late or I get billing confused, it creates these tense moments with the staff and the customers, and then I feel like I’m just trying to catch up instead of moving things forward.\n\nAnd church is the other one. I used to help with planning more, and I liked that—well, I liked being useful, I guess. But I stepped back from leading some of that because people were getting frustrated with me. Not always saying it outright, but you can tell. And then I think, “Well, maybe I shouldn’t take that on,” even when part of me wants to. So yes, I think it has kept me from doing things I wanted to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or missing details in your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Like with the dry-cleaning orders, I can sit down and start checking them just fine, and I think, “Okay, I’m going to be careful this time.” But then after a few minutes my mind is somewhere else—like I’ll notice a label, or I’ll think about choir plans at church, or something I need to do at home—and then I come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless. I’ll feel like I was working on it the whole time, but I guess my attention wandered and I didn’t catch the details.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with organization and focus significantly impacted your ability to carry out daily tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, the everyday responsibilities at the shop are where it shows the most. If I don’t keep things organized, orders can go out late, or I mix up billing, and then it turns into this whole tense thing with staff and customers. And then I’m thinking about it all day, even when I’m supposed to be doing the next thing.\n\nAt church too—well, church planning has a lot of little pieces, you know, who’s bringing what, what time things start, all that—and I used to help lead some of that. But I stepped back because people were getting frustrated with me. Not yelling or anything, but I could tell. So yes, it has definitely affected how I carry out responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with organization and focus as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, it wasn’t called anything back then, at least not that I remember. But in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered. That was the word I remember hearing.\n\nI’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d lose papers—homework sheets, permission slips, all that kind of thing. And I needed reminders to finish my work. My mother would get those notes or hear it at conferences, and then she’d come home and say, “You have to pay attention,” and I really thought I was trying. I just… didn’t seem to stay with it the way other kids did.\n\nDoctor: Do you struggle with organization and focus in situations where there are fewer distractions, such as when you're working on a task alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, even when I’m by myself. I’ll sit down with the day’s dry-cleaning orders and think, “Okay, just go through these one by one,” and I can start out fine. But after a few minutes, my mind is off on something else—like the labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home.\n\nAnd then I come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to check. It’s not always because people are interrupting me. Sometimes it’s just me drifting off, even in a quiet room.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with organization and focus consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s been pretty consistent, I think. I mean, looking back, even in grade school my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but scattered. I remember that because it kind of stuck with me.\n\nI was always talking when I shouldn’t, losing papers—homework sheets, forms, things like that—and needing reminders to finish my work. And then as an adult it’s just… different papers, different responsibilities, but the same sort of struggle staying organized and keeping my focus where it’s supposed to be.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Then I’m standing there not knowing what I’m supposed to handle first.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing, honestly, because I used to have my little way of doing things. I mean, everybody at work has their own system, you know, like one lady keeps everything clipped together with these colored clips—she’s very particular about it—but mine just turns into a mess before I even realize it. So… yeah, that’s why I came. It’s getting hard to manage.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble keeping things organized at home as well, or is this mainly a problem at work?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly work that made me come in, I think. I mean, at home I have the usual piles like anybody, mail on the counter and that sort of thing, but at work it’s worse because there are tickets, receipts, little notes from the workshop, customer jobs coming in… and they all just end up in one pile.\n\nThen I’m looking at it like, okay, what was I supposed to do first? And someone will ask about an order, and I’m digging through papers. The other day I found a receipt stuck under a stack of cleaning slips—well, I think it was a receipt, or maybe a workshop note, that’s the problem. It all blends together. So yes, mainly work, that’s where it’s really causing trouble.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while trying to work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll sit down to go through the dry-cleaning orders for the day, and at first I’m fine, I’m checking the slips and the labels and all that. But then a few minutes in, my mind just… wanders off. I’ll start thinking about the labels themselves, or something with choir plans, or what I need to do when I get home.\n\nAnd then I sort of snap back and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking. It’s frustrating because I was looking right at it, you know? But I wasn’t really taking it in.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think of myself as someone who’s bouncing around all the time or anything. Sitting still… well, it depends, I guess. At work I’m usually on my feet anyway, moving between the counter and the back, so I don’t really notice it there.\n\nIf I’m sitting at home, I might shift around or mess with something in my hands, but I don’t know if that’s more than anyone else. My sister does this thing where she taps her nails on the table, and it drives me crazy, so I try not to do things like that. But… yeah, I’m not sure I’ve paid much attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed by your difficulties with organization and distraction?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Overwhelmed maybe, sometimes, when everything is stacked up and I can’t tell what’s what. But then I think, well, everybody gets behind at work, right?\n\nIt’s more like I get irritated with myself. Like I’ll be staring at the pile and then remember I was supposed to call someone back, and then—oh, that reminds me, we had this one customer last week who kept changing what day she wanted pickup, and it threw everything off. So then the whole counter was full of slips, and I kept thinking I’d sort it after lunch, but lunch came and went.\n\nSo… yes, maybe overwhelmed at times, but I’m not sure if I’d say anxious. I’m not really sure where the line is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the time. I mean, I have days where I’m just worn out or in a mood, especially if work’s been a mess, but sad like all day, every day? I wouldn’t really say that.\n\nSometimes I get annoyed with myself more than anything. Like yesterday I meant to stop for groceries and then somehow ended up coming straight home, and then I was mad because I had to figure out dinner with nothing in the house. But that’s not exactly sad, I guess. So… no, I don’t think sad most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with organization and staying focused, or is this something that's developed over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly. I don’t know if it was exactly the same as now, but even back in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright but scattered. That was the phrase they used a lot, I think.\n\nThey’d say I was talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d lose papers, or I needed reminders to finish my work. My mother would get so frustrated because she’d say, “You know how to do it, why didn’t you turn it in?” And I did know how, usually, but then the paper would be in my desk or my bag or… who knows.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think it just suddenly developed. It’s kind of always been part of me, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. Not like I’m doing wild things, but I jump in too fast. Like my daughter will be asking me something and I’m already answering before she’s finished, and then she’ll say, “That’s not what I asked,” and I realize I was answering what I thought she meant.\n\nIt happens with customers too. They start asking about a pickup or a stain or whatever, and I’ll jump in with the answer before they get the whole sentence out. And then sometimes I’ve answered the wrong thing and have to back up. Same with the trainer at work—though training is hard anyway because they explain things in all these steps, and there was this one machine that kept beeping last week—anyway, yes, I do tend to answer before I’ve really heard the whole question.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with organization and focus affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I think. More at work and church than with friends, maybe, but it spills over.\n\nAt the shop, when I’m not organized, orders can run late or I mix up little details, like billing things, and then everybody’s tense because the customer’s waiting and the staff are looking at me like, “What happened?” And I hate that feeling. It makes me want to explain myself, but then I probably make it longer than it needs to be.\n\nAnd at church too—I used to help lead some of the planning stuff, like for events, and I’ve kind of stepped back because people were getting frustrated with me. Not in a mean way, exactly, but you can tell when folks are tired of reminding you or fixing things. So yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a day off work or cancel plans due to feeling overwhelmed by your disorganization or difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve taken a whole day off just for that, exactly… I mean, sometimes I’ve wanted to. At the shop it can build up, you know? Like one order is late, then the billing is wrong on another one, and then a customer is standing there waiting and the staff are all tense, and I’m trying to fix it but I can’t seem to get everything straight in my head.\n\nAnd then at church, I actually did step back from helping lead some of the planning things, because people were getting frustrated with me. Not yelling or anything, just… you can feel it when people are tired of reminding you or cleaning up after the mix-ups. So I guess I have canceled or stepped away from plans more than taken a sick day, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family or friends it’s not always as obvious as at work, but it does affect people around me. At the shop, when I’m disorganized, orders have been late, or I’ve gotten billing mixed up, and then everybody gets tense—staff, customers, me too. It kind of follows me home because I’m still upset about it.\n\nAnd at church, I stepped back from leading some of the planning things because people were getting frustrated with me. Not like anyone was cruel, but you can tell when they’re tired of fixing things or reminding you. So… yes, it has affected relationships, even if it shows up more in those settings than just sitting with a friend over coffee or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your difficulties with organization and focus have held you back from achieving your goals or doing things you want to do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel like it’s held me back. I don’t know if I’d say it in some big dramatic way, but at the shop, when orders end up late or I get billing confused, it creates these tense moments with the staff and the customers, and then I feel like I’m just trying to catch up instead of moving things forward.\n\nAnd church is the other one. I used to help with planning more, and I liked that—well, I liked being useful, I guess. But I stepped back from leading some of that because people were getting frustrated with me. Not always saying it outright, but you can tell. And then I think, “Well, maybe I shouldn’t take that on,” even when part of me wants to. So yes, I think it has kept me from doing things I wanted to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or missing details in your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Like with the dry-cleaning orders, I can sit down and start checking them just fine, and I think, “Okay, I’m going to be careful this time.” But then after a few minutes my mind is somewhere else—like I’ll notice a label, or I’ll think about choir plans at church, or something I need to do at home—and then I come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless. I’ll feel like I was working on it the whole time, but I guess my attention wandered and I didn’t catch the details.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with organization and focus significantly impacted your ability to carry out daily tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, the everyday responsibilities at the shop are where it shows the most. If I don’t keep things organized, orders can go out late, or I mix up billing, and then it turns into this whole tense thing with staff and customers. And then I’m thinking about it all day, even when I’m supposed to be doing the next thing.\n\nAt church too—well, church planning has a lot of little pieces, you know, who’s bringing what, what time things start, all that—and I used to help lead some of that. But I stepped back because people were getting frustrated with me. Not yelling or anything, but I could tell. So yes, it has definitely affected how I carry out responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with organization and focus as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. That was the word I remember hearing.\n\nI’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d lose papers—homework sheets, permission slips, all that kind of thing. And I needed reminders to finish my work. My mother would get those notes or hear it at conferences, and then she’d come home and say, “You have to pay attention,” and I really thought I was trying. I just… didn’t seem to stay with it the way other kids did.\n\nDoctor: Do you struggle with organization and focus in situations where there are fewer distractions, such as when you're working on a task alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, even when I’m by myself. But… yeah, I’m not sure I’ve paid much attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed by your difficulties with organization and distraction?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Overwhelmed maybe, sometimes, when everything is stacked up and I can’t tell what’s what. But then I think, well, everybody gets behind at work, right?\n\nIt’s more like I get irritated with myself. Like I’ll be staring at the pile and then remember I was supposed to call someone back, and then—oh, that reminds me, we had this one customer last week who kept changing what day she wanted pickup, and it threw everything off. So then the whole counter was full of slips, and I kept thinking I’d sort it after lunch, but lunch came and went.\n\nSo… yes, maybe overwhelmed at times, but I’m not sure if I’d say anxious. I’m not really sure where the line is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the time. I mean, I have days where I’m just worn out or in a mood, especially if work’s been a mess, but sad like all day, every day? I wouldn’t really say that.\n\nSometimes I get annoyed with myself more than anything. Like yesterday I meant to stop for groceries and then somehow ended up coming straight home, and then I was mad because I had to figure out dinner with nothing in the house. But that’s not exactly sad, I guess. So… no, I don’t think sad most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with organization and staying focused, or is this something that's developed over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly. I don’t know if it was exactly the same as now, but even back in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright but scattered. That was the phrase they used a lot, I think.\n\nThey’d say I was talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d lose papers, or I needed reminders to finish my work. My mother would get so frustrated because she’d say, “You know how to do it, why didn’t you turn it in?” And I did know how, usually, but then the paper would be in my desk or my bag or… who knows.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think it just suddenly developed. It’s kind of always been part of me, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. Not like I’m doing wild things, but I jump in too fast. Like my daughter will be asking me something and I’m already answering before she’s finished, and then she’ll say, “That’s not what I asked,” and I realize I was answering what I thought she meant.\n\nIt happens with customers too. They start asking about a pickup or a stain or whatever, and I’ll jump in with the answer before they get the whole sentence out. And then sometimes I’ve answered the wrong thing and have to back up. Same with the trainer at work—though training is hard anyway because they explain things in all these steps, and there was this one machine that kept beeping last week—anyway, yes, I do tend to answer before I’ve really heard the whole question.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with organization and focus affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I think. More at work and church than with friends, maybe, but it spills over.\n\nAt the shop, when I’m not organized, orders can run late or I mix up little details, like billing things, and then everybody’s tense because the customer’s waiting and the staff are looking at me like, “What happened?” And I hate that feeling. It makes me want to explain myself, but then I probably make it longer than it needs to be.\n\nAnd at church too—I used to help lead some of the planning stuff, like for events, and I’ve kind of stepped back because people were getting frustrated with me. Not in a mean way, exactly, but you can tell when folks are tired of reminding you or fixing things. So yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a day off work or cancel plans due to feeling overwhelmed by your disorganization or difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve taken a whole day off just for that, exactly… I mean, sometimes I’ve wanted to. At the shop it can build up, you know? Like one order is late, then the billing is wrong on another one, and then a customer is standing there waiting and the staff are all tense, and I’m trying to fix it but I can’t seem to get everything straight in my head.\n\nAnd then at church, I actually did step back from helping lead some of the planning things, because people were getting frustrated with me. Not yelling or anything, just… you can feel it when people are tired of reminding you or cleaning up after the mix-ups. So I guess I have canceled or stepped away from plans more than taken a sick day, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family or friends it’s not always as obvious as at work, but it does affect people around me. At the shop, when I’m disorganized, orders have been late, or I’ve gotten billing mixed up, and then everybody gets tense—staff, customers, me too. It kind of follows me home because I’m still upset about it.\n\nAnd at church, I stepped back from leading some of the planning things because people were getting frustrated with me. Not like anyone was cruel, but you can tell when they’re tired of fixing things or reminding you. So… yes, it has affected relationships, even if it shows up more in those settings than just sitting with a friend over coffee or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your difficulties with organization and focus have held you back from achieving your goals or doing things you want to do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel like it’s held me back. I don’t know if I’d say it in some big dramatic way, but at the shop, when orders end up late or I get billing confused, it creates these tense moments with the staff and the customers, and then I feel like I’m just trying to catch up instead of moving things forward.\n\nAnd church is the other one. I used to help with planning more, and I liked that—well, I liked being useful, I guess. But I stepped back from leading some of that because people were getting frustrated with me. Not always saying it outright, but you can tell. And then I think, “Well, maybe I shouldn’t take that on,” even when part of me wants to. So yes, I think it has kept me from doing things I wanted to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or missing details in your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Like with the dry-cleaning orders, I can sit down and start checking them just fine, and I think, “Okay, I’m going to be careful this time.” But then after a few minutes my mind is somewhere else—like I’ll notice a label, or I’ll think about choir plans at church, or something I need to do at home—and then I come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless. I’ll feel like I was working on it the whole time, but I guess my attention wandered and I didn’t catch the details.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with organization and focus significantly impacted your ability to carry out daily tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, the everyday responsibilities at the shop are where it shows the most. If I don’t keep things organized, orders can go out late, or I mix up billing, and then it turns into this whole tense thing with staff and customers. And then I’m thinking about it all day, even when I’m supposed to be doing the next thing.\n\nAt church too—well, church planning has a lot of little pieces, you know, who’s bringing what, what time things start, all that—and I used to help lead some of that. But I stepped back because people were getting frustrated with me. Not yelling or anything, but I could tell. So yes, it has definitely affected how I carry out responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with organization and focus as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, it wasn’t called anything back then, at least not that I remember. But in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered. That was the word I remember hearing.\n\nI’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d lose papers—homework sheets, permission slips, all that kind of thing. And I needed reminders to finish my work. My mother would get those notes or hear it at conferences, and then she’d come home and say, “You have to pay attention,” and I really thought I was trying. I just… didn’t seem to stay with it the way other kids did.\n\nDoctor: Do you struggle with organization and focus in situations where there are fewer distractions, such as when you're working on a task alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, even when I’m by myself. I’ll sit down with the day’s dry-cleaning orders and think, “Okay, just go through these one by one,” and I can start out fine. But after a few minutes, my mind is off on something else—like the labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home.\n\nAnd then I come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to check. It’s not always because people are interrupting me. Sometimes it’s just me drifting off, even in a quiet room.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with organization and focus consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s been pretty consistent, I think. I mean, looking back, even in grade school my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but scattered. I remember that because it kind of stuck with me.\n\nI was always talking when I shouldn’t, losing papers—homework sheets, forms, things like that—and needing reminders to finish my work. And then as an adult it’s just… different papers, different responsibilities, but the same sort of struggle staying organized and keeping my focus where it’s supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you always struggled with staying organized and focused, from your childhood up to the present?\n\nPatient: Yes, pretty much. I know I’ve kind of said this already, but even back in grade school my teachers would tell my mother I was “bright but scattered.” That phrase really stuck with me.\n\nI was the kid talking when I wasn’t supposed to, losing papers all the time—homework, forms, whatever—and needing reminders to finish things. And honestly, that same pattern has just sort of followed me along, even if the details changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or restless, even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do move around a lot at work, so it’s hard to tell what’s restlessness and what’s just the job. At the counter or in the back, you’re always reaching for something, sorting things, checking tags—well, when I remember where I put the tag sheets.\n\nBut sitting still… I don’t know. I might tap my foot sometimes, but I haven’t really paid attention to it like that. My husband might notice more than I do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

At work I’m usually on my feet anyway, moving between the counter and the back, so I don’t really notice it there.\n\nIf I’m sitting at home, I might shift around or mess with something in my hands, but I don’t know if that’s more than anyone else. My sister does this thing where she taps her nails on the table, and it drives me crazy, so I try not to do things like that. But… yeah, I’m not sure I’ve paid much attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed by your difficulties with organization and distraction?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. Overwhelmed maybe, sometimes, when everything is stacked up and I can’t tell what’s what. But then I think, well, everybody gets behind at work, right?\n\nIt’s more like I get irritated with myself. Like I’ll be staring at the pile and then remember I was supposed to call someone back, and then—oh, that reminds me, we had this one customer last week who kept changing what day she wanted pickup, and it threw everything off. So then the whole counter was full of slips, and I kept thinking I’d sort it after lunch, but lunch came and went.\n\nSo… yes, maybe overwhelmed at times, but I’m not sure if I’d say anxious. I’m not really sure where the line is.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or down most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not most of the time. I mean, I have days where I’m just worn out or in a mood, especially if work’s been a mess, but sad like all day, every day? I wouldn’t really say that.\n\nSometimes I get annoyed with myself more than anything. Like yesterday I meant to stop for groceries and then somehow ended up coming straight home, and then I was mad because I had to figure out dinner with nothing in the house. But that’s not exactly sad, I guess. So… no, I don’t think sad most of the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with organization and staying focused, or is this something that's developed over time?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been there a long time, honestly. I don’t know if it was exactly the same as now, but even back in grade school my teachers used to tell my mother I was bright but scattered. That was the phrase they used a lot, I think.\n\nThey’d say I was talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d lose papers, or I needed reminders to finish my work. My mother would get so frustrated because she’d say, “You know how to do it, why didn’t you turn it in?” And I did know how, usually, but then the paper would be in my desk or my bag or… who knows.\n\nSo, no, I don’t think it just suddenly developed. It’s kind of always been part of me, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do sometimes. Not like I’m doing wild things, but I jump in too fast. Like my daughter will be asking me something and I’m already answering before she’s finished, and then she’ll say, “That’s not what I asked,” and I realize I was answering what I thought she meant.\n\nIt happens with customers too. They start asking about a pickup or a stain or whatever, and I’ll jump in with the answer before they get the whole sentence out. And then sometimes I’ve answered the wrong thing and have to back up. Same with the trainer at work—though training is hard anyway because they explain things in all these steps, and there was this one machine that kept beeping last week—anyway, yes, I do tend to answer before I’ve really heard the whole question.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with organization and focus affected your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I think. More at work and church than with friends, maybe, but it spills over.\n\nAt the shop, when I’m not organized, orders can run late or I mix up little details, like billing things, and then everybody’s tense because the customer’s waiting and the staff are looking at me like, “What happened?” And I hate that feeling. It makes me want to explain myself, but then I probably make it longer than it needs to be.\n\nAnd at church too—I used to help lead some of the planning stuff, like for events, and I’ve kind of stepped back because people were getting frustrated with me. Not in a mean way, exactly, but you can tell when folks are tired of reminding you or fixing things. So yes, it’s affected things.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had to take a day off work or cancel plans due to feeling overwhelmed by your disorganization or difficulty focusing?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’ve taken a whole day off just for that, exactly… I mean, sometimes I’ve wanted to. At the shop it can build up, you know? Like one order is late, then the billing is wrong on another one, and then a customer is standing there waiting and the staff are all tense, and I’m trying to fix it but I can’t seem to get everything straight in my head.\n\nAnd then at church, I actually did step back from helping lead some of the planning things, because people were getting frustrated with me. Not yelling or anything, just… you can feel it when people are tired of reminding you or cleaning up after the mix-ups. So I guess I have canceled or stepped away from plans more than taken a sick day, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your difficulties with organization and focus affecting your personal relationships with friends or family members?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, with family or friends it’s not always as obvious as at work, but it does affect people around me. At the shop, when I’m disorganized, orders have been late, or I’ve gotten billing mixed up, and then everybody gets tense—staff, customers, me too. It kind of follows me home because I’m still upset about it.\n\nAnd at church, I stepped back from leading some of the planning things because people were getting frustrated with me. Not like anyone was cruel, but you can tell when they’re tired of fixing things or reminding you. So… yes, it has affected relationships, even if it shows up more in those settings than just sitting with a friend over coffee or something.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like your difficulties with organization and focus have held you back from achieving your goals or doing things you want to do?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do feel like it’s held me back. I don’t know if I’d say it in some big dramatic way, but at the shop, when orders end up late or I get billing confused, it creates these tense moments with the staff and the customers, and then I feel like I’m just trying to catch up instead of moving things forward.\n\nAnd church is the other one. I used to help with planning more, and I liked that—well, I liked being useful, I guess. But I stepped back from leading some of that because people were getting frustrated with me. Not always saying it outright, but you can tell. And then I think, “Well, maybe I shouldn’t take that on,” even when part of me wants to. So yes, I think it has kept me from doing things I wanted to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself making careless mistakes or missing details in your work or daily activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Like with the dry-cleaning orders, I can sit down and start checking them just fine, and I think, “Okay, I’m going to be careful this time.” But then after a few minutes my mind is somewhere else—like I’ll notice a label, or I’ll think about choir plans at church, or something I need to do at home—and then I come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to be checking.\n\nIt’s frustrating because I’m not trying to be careless. I’ll feel like I was working on it the whole time, but I guess my attention wandered and I didn’t catch the details.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with organization and focus significantly impacted your ability to carry out daily tasks and responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, the everyday responsibilities at the shop are where it shows the most. If I don’t keep things organized, orders can go out late, or I mix up billing, and then it turns into this whole tense thing with staff and customers. And then I’m thinking about it all day, even when I’m supposed to be doing the next thing.\n\nAt church too—well, church planning has a lot of little pieces, you know, who’s bringing what, what time things start, all that—and I used to help lead some of that. But I stepped back because people were getting frustrated with me. Not yelling or anything, but I could tell. So yes, it has definitely affected how I carry out responsibilities.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience similar difficulties with organization and focus as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, it wasn’t called anything back then, at least not that I remember. But in grade school, my teachers would tell my mother I was bright, but scattered. That was the word I remember hearing.\n\nI’d be talking when I wasn’t supposed to, or I’d lose papers—homework sheets, permission slips, all that kind of thing. And I needed reminders to finish my work. My mother would get those notes or hear it at conferences, and then she’d come home and say, “You have to pay attention,” and I really thought I was trying. I just… didn’t seem to stay with it the way other kids did.\n\nDoctor: Do you struggle with organization and focus in situations where there are fewer distractions, such as when you're working on a task alone?\n\nPatient: Yes, even when I’m by myself. I’ll sit down with the day’s dry-cleaning orders and think, “Okay, just go through these one by one,” and I can start out fine. But after a few minutes, my mind is off on something else—like the labels, or choir plans, or something I need to do at home.\n\nAnd then I come back to it and realize I missed half of what I was supposed to check. It’s not always because people are interrupting me. Sometimes it’s just me drifting off, even in a quiet room.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced difficulties with organization and focus consistently throughout your childhood and into adulthood?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s been pretty consistent, I think. I mean, looking back, even in grade school my teachers would tell my mother I was bright but scattered. I remember that because it kind of stuck with me.\n\nI was always talking when I shouldn’t, losing papers—homework sheets, forms, things like that—and needing reminders to finish my work. And then as an adult it’s just… different papers, different responsibilities, but the same sort of struggle staying organized and keeping my focus where it’s supposed to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you always struggled with staying organized and focused, from your childhood up to the present?\n\nPatient: Yes, pretty much. I know I’ve kind of said this already, but even back in grade school my teachers would tell my mother I was “bright but scattered.” That phrase really stuck with me.\n\nI was the kid talking when I wasn’t supposed to, losing papers all the time—homework, forms, whatever—and needing reminders to finish things. And honestly, that same pattern has just sort of followed me along, even if the details changed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're fidgeting or restless, even when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I mean, I do move around a lot at work, so it’s hard to tell what’s restlessness and what’s just the job. At the counter or in the back, you’re always reaching for something, sorting things, checking tags—well, when I remember where I put the tag sheets.\n\nBut sitting still… I don’t know. I might tap my foot sometimes, but I haven’t really paid attention to it like that. My husband might notice more than I do.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to fulfill responsibilities at work or at home due to your difficulties with organization and focus?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, it has caused problems. At work, there have been late orders because I didn’t keep things straight, or I thought something was done and it wasn’t. And billing can get mixed up, which is embarrassing because then a customer is standing there upset and I’m trying to figure out where I put the slip or what I wrote down.\n\nIt’s not every single day, but enough that the staff gets tense with me sometimes. And outside of work too—at church I used to help lead some planning things, but I’ve stepped back from that because people were getting frustrated with me. I’d miss details or not keep the pieces organized, and then someone else would have to clean it up."
